ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U1	The next item is the first reading of the draft law amending the State Assets Act initiated by the Government of the Republic. Initiator waitress Secretary of Finance Sven Sester, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U2	Hi! So let's discuss a bill bearing number 455. For all those who do not know what is behind this number, this is a draft law amending the State Assets Act. Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! The Estonian state currently owns a stake in 31 companies operating in Estonia. These assets already amounted to EUR 6.4 billion by the end of 2016, with a total turnover of EUR 1.5 billion. Profits amounted to EUR 241 million, 22 companies discontinued year-on-year profit and nine losses. Last year, the State received dividends from companies amounting to EUR 95 million. This year we expect dividends to reach EUR 137 million. The Government of the Republic has submitted to you a draft law amending the State Assets Act, which we want to improve the quality of the management of companies and thus improve the transparency and effectiveness of their activities. The amendments to the draft can be divided into three blocks: changes in the appointment of members of the supervisory board, increased transparency of companies' activities and changes in the regulation of subsidies paid by companies. I'll give you an overview of all these three blocks in more substance at this fine hour of the night. Firstly, for the appointment of members of the competent council, the Government of the Republic is required to set up a nomination committee. This will, according to experts, address the most important lack of governance of public undertakings. So far, the election of the members of the Governing Council has not been sufficiently systematic, competent or transparent. In the model of the nomination committee for larger stock exchange companies, senior managers recognised by the private sector play a key role in the selection of board members. The appointment committee shall propose to the dominant minister the size of the Council's composition, the specific candidates for the members of the Council and the remuneration to be paid to them. The nomination committee is already operating, but it is doing so as a temporary committee under the Government of the Republic Act. By amending the State Assets Act, he becomes a permanent advisory body for ministers. To date, the nomination committee has been able to propose to ministers the election of the members of the board of directors of ten companies. The draft also specifies the rules for avoiding conflicts of interest for a member of the Governing Council. The assessment of conflicts of interest must be substantive, not formal. The evaluation shall also include the interests of the persons concerned with the candidate for a member of the Governing Council. The draft provides that when major public undertakings disclose their activities, similar requirements to those applicable to those listed companies apply. This means that information so far created and used for internal management will also provide the public with a more comprehensive overview of the company. Until now, companies have been obliged to disclose their financial results only in numerical form, and in future they will have to explain where they have achieved their objectives and what and how they have affected them. The larger companies will also have to inform the public immediately about the circumstances or events which have a significant impact on their activities. Increased public attention and a better understanding of companies' activities will motivate companies to act more effectively. Further disclosure requirements will be mandatory for 11 public undertakings, which are also subject to the obligation to set up an audit committee. A fundamental change will take place in the rules on the payment of grants to companies. The current practice of granting grants has been that the subsidies paid have often not been linked to the company's core business. It is therefore necessary to organise the practice and focus, and grants may be paid to an area from which the greatest contribution to the achievement of the company's objectives can be expected. The government estimates this area to be R &amp; D. The draft restricts the payment of subsidies to the R &amp; D activities of the company. The amount of the subsidy shall be reduced to 0,5% of the average consolidated net profit of the three preceding financial years of the company. This will allow for increased dividends and other areas to be financed through the state budget in a transparent manner. As I conclude, the amendments to the draft are reflected in the desire to improve the governance of public undertakings. Professional councils set up by the nomination committee play a major role in this respect, as well as in enhancing the transparency of companies' activities. These are long-prepared changes based on recommendations made by private and public sector experts on the governance of public companies. Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U31	The next item is the first reading of the draft law on ratification of the Rail Baltic/Rail Baltica Agreement between the Government of the Republic of Estonia, the Government of the Republic of Lithuania and the Government of the Republic of Latvia, initiated by the Government of the Republic of Estonia. The initiator's waitress is Kadri Simson, Minister for Economic Affairs and Infrastructure. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U32	Dear Chairman of the Riigikogu! Happy Members of the Riigikogu! If the Minister of Finance, Sven Sester, held a discussion with you for hours and hours, probably because he had many new ideas to introduce you, then I will be in front of you with an old and long-discussed subject. It can be said that the topic of Rail Baltic has been discussed in Estonia for years or even decades. But I will now be more specific about this agreement, which the Government of the Republic gave the Prime Minister a mandate to sign, and which the Prime Minister of Estonia, Latvia and Lithuania signed on 31 January inclusive. Just as any international agreement needs ratification in the Riigikogu. Transport connections between the Baltic States, including Estonia, and other Europe are essential for the development of these countries. Modern, fast and efficient connections can be considered as the basis for both social and economic development. Rail Baltic will be part of the trans-European road network defined by Regulation No 1315/2013 of the European Parliament and of the Council. More specifically, the priorities for the development of the TEN-T network are set out in Regulation No 1316/2013 of the European Parliament and of the Council. These regulations set out the network sections with the highest priority in European terms, the transport corridors of the TEN-T core network. Estonia is covered by the North Sea-Baltic Core Network Corridor, which includes not only Rail Baltic but also Via Baltica, Tallinn and Muuga ports and Tallinn airport. The development of international transport corridors will make it possible to increase the internal coherence and regional balance of the country. Rail Baltic is not a separate railway, but part of the Estonian transport network. All residents of Estonia can use the fast travel connection if they wish. The existing railway will be connected to Rail Baltic primarily through the terminal built in Tallinn for Ülemiste and the multimodal terminal planned for Muuga and built in the framework of the Rail Baltic project and the planned freight station in Pärnu. As Rail Baltic's future travel and freight transport depends on the development of the entire transport network in Estonia, it is important to look at it in the context of the entire transport infrastructure, whose balanced development is supported by earlier and recent decisions on infrastructure investments. In the Rail Baltic route, the three Baltic States agreed years ago, first in 1994 to approve the Baltic region's vision for 2010. Previous agreements have been based on further decisions of the European Commission, the other Baltic States and also the Republic of Estonia, for example the intergovernmental agreement relating to the construction of Rail Baltic on 31 January. This agreement has been sent for ratification by the governments of the three Baltic States to their national parliaments. The Government of the Republic of Estonia linked the agreement to the completion of a feasibility and cost-benefit analysis based on updated data sent to the Riigikogu. The results of the analysis are already available to members of the Riigikogu as well as to the public since the beginning of May this year. By the law in question, the Republic of Estonia ratifies the intergovernmental agreement on the development of the Rail Baltic railway connection. This agreement establishes as a legally binding instrument the obligations of the countries that need to be fulfilled so that Rail Baltic railway connections can be established with Central Europe through the Baltic States. The agreement determines the overall technical parameters of this project, the time frame for the railway route and the time frame for the construction of the railway. In addition, the pre-conditions for the construction of Rail Baltic, their performer, the role of RB Rail AS in the fulfilment of the preconditions and the construction of the railway until full operational availability is reached. The agreement also regulates the infrastructure to be built and the ownership of the land under it, as well as the conditions for financing the construction. In addition, access to Rail Baltic infrastructure will be agreed and general guidelines will be given for the management of Rail Baltic for the designation of an infrastructure manager. The Treaty will be ratified by the Riigikogu pursuant to § 121 (4) of the Constitution of the Republic of Estonia, according to which the Riigikogu will ratify and denounce the treaties of the Republic of Estonia whereby the state will assume property obligations. Upon ratification and entry into force of the interstate agreement, the Estonian state will have a separate international obligation to Latvia and Lithuania to complete the Rail Baltic railway by 2025 and to start using the rail link in 2026. The agreement does not create, alter or terminate the obligations of the Republic of Estonia towards the European Union. The same commitment is made by Latvia and Lithuania before Estonia and the European Union. The existence or absence of a transnational agreement may have a significant impact on the success of the following grants from CEF. The CEF Regulation for the current financial period provides that actions in the field of transport involving a cross-border paragraph or parts thereof may receive Union financial assistance only if a written agreement has been concluded between those countries for the completion of the cross-border sections. During this budgetary period of the European Union, the Commission has considered the joint declarations made in the applications, the operational level agreements concluded between RB Rail AS and the Baltic States and the joint letter of support drawn up by the governments of the Baltic States to be sufficient for the grant, assuming that the process of concluding the Rail Baltic transnational agreement to be included in the CEF applications will be effectively completed and the agreement will be concluded and enter into force. It is also important to bear in mind that the role of an appropriate international agreement places high priority on the preconditions for EU support both in the current and in the forthcoming funding period. On 11 May, the Act on the ratification of the Agreement already passed a first reading in the Parliament of the Republic of Latvia, where it obtained 73 votes in favour, no abstentions and no votes against. The cost-benefit study of Rail Baltic was carried out between October 2015 and March 2017. The Estonian Government had decided that ratification would be expected in Estonia until the results of this study became public. The purpose of the cost-benefit study was to determine the economic justification for the construction of the Rail Baltic project, the Warsaw-Tallinna route, in line with common principles, of a new, fast and European standard gauge rail link, based on up-to-date data. The results of the study will form the basis for further financing of the project. The analysis shows that the Rail Baltic project is a profitable infrastructure investment for Estonian society as a whole. The project is profitable from a socio-economic point of view, as its economic benefits go beyond economic losses. The economic viability of the project depends on how the main advantages of the project are secured: speed of train movement, environmental impacts, use of local labour and materials. Market analysis and traffic forecasts show that Rail Baltic has potential for both passenger and freight transport. The total cost of the project is estimated at approximately 5.8 billion euros, of which 1.345 billion will be invested on Estonian territory. The financial needs, i.e. the difference between the costs of the investment and the revenues generated by the new railway, have been calculated. This is 94,18% of the total investment cost, which, in the case of investments from Estonia, means EUR 268 million of the state's own contribution to the construction of Rail Baltic. With a discount rate of 5%, the project has a positive net present value of EUR 879 million, at basic prices for 2015. The benefit-cost ratio is 1,19. The corresponding internal rate of return is 6,32%. Without EU funding, investment and financial indicators reflect negative results, stressing the importance of ensuring EU funding. I will also stop for a moment in the context of socio-economic income. For both Rail Baltic and other infrastructure objects built from public funds, it is important to bear in mind that the revenue generated by the object is mainly socio-economic in nature. All major infrastructure sites, whether roads, railways or water networks, must meet broader objectives than generating direct financial revenue from infrastructure. In the event of a direct high and rapid financial return, the infrastructure would be set up by private companies wishing to make a quick return on the money invested in the infrastructure. If entrepreneurs are not ready to do so, the state must take part. However, in the case of strategic investments, the European Union has created opportunities to earn support for infrastructure. It is important for Estonia that people living in peripheral areas also have a permanent connection with the rest of the country, and Europe that the peoples living in peripheral areas of Europe have a permanent link with the centres of Europe. To put it simply, the prerequisite for investment, both with state support and with European Union support, is that a private operator is mostly not prepared to finance such projects in full. In the case of infrastructure, it is important that the owner is able to maintain it in such a condition that the built road or railway can be used for its intended purpose. Essentially, this means that sufficient resources must be planned to maintain the object while it is being used. The money intended for the maintenance of state roads comes directly from the state budget in Estonia each year. Tax revenue is used, i.e. indirectly, all Estonian taxpayers pay for the maintenance of the entire Estonian road network, even if this person is not driving and does not even have a driving licence. Railways offer a considerable alternative, as rail charges are paid by both freight and passenger carriers, which means that most of the costs are borne by the user of the infrastructure. Rail Baltic must be seen as the way we have planned this project in cooperation with the three Baltic States and the European Commission in particular: fast and environmentally friendly transport infrastructure that connects the three Baltic States and Finland with each other and with other Europe. The task of the state is to ensure the performance of this infrastructure and its compliance with national objectives, for example by better connecting Tallinn, Rapla and Pärnu. It is also important to mitigate the potential negative environmental impacts of infrastructure building. There is reason to confirm that we are on the right track in this direction. This is confirmed, for example, by the recent decision of the Environmental Board to coordinate the Strategic Environmental Assessment of the Rail Baltic project. This year, the introduction by de jure rural elders of the Rail Baltic de facto route is still pending. In the same way as other transport infrastructure, Rail Baltic can be considered as an enabler. One railway or road cannot be an end in itself; it is a means of achieving something bigger, whether it is thanks to better transport connections that additional investments are coming to Estonia and the resulting economic benefits or the Renaissance of the environmentally friendly mode of transport and the lives saved. Thank you for your attention.

Kadri Simson, Minister of Economic Affairs and Infrastructure, Ahti King, Deputy Chancellor of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications, Anti Moppel, Adviser to the same Ministry, Riil Baltic coordinator Kristjan Kaunissaare, and Adviser to the Fiscal Policy Department of the Ministry of Finance, Mari Lahtmets, were invited. The Commission discussed this issue and decided to send the draft to its first reading today. At the same time, the Commission also decided that the draft will not be sent to second reading until the Commission has received the opinion of the Chancellor of Justice and there is an audit by the National Audit Office and a clear order of budget and execution so that all members of the Riigikogu know exactly what they are ratifying, what is being done in Estonia for this money. The Commission took the following decisions: to propose to appoint a speaker (consensually) before you as rapporteur for the commission, to put the draft on the plenary agenda today (by 8 members and 1 was against), to propose that the first reading be closed (consensually) and to propose that the deadline for tabling amendments be set at 5 p.m. on 31 May (consensually). ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U123	Good Vice-Chairman of the Riigikogu! Good morning, good members of the Riigikogu. There is a very short but very important draft before you, the Act amending the Foundations of the Property Reform Act of the Republic of Estonia. The following amendments are made to the Act on the Foundations of the Property Reform of the Republic of Estonia. First, Paragraph 12 (71) inserts the following third and fourth sentences: 'If operations cannot be carried out for any other reason beyond the control of the entitled subject, the Minister responsible for the field shall set the deadline for completion of the operations. The Minister may authorise the appointment of a deadline to an official of his own responsibility. ' Secondly, Paragraph 13 (8) inserts the following third and fourth sentences: 'If the decision on compensation cannot be taken for any other reason beyond the control of the entitled subject, the time limit for taking the decision shall be set by the Minister responsible for the field. The Minister may authorise the appointment of a deadline to an official of his own responsibility. ' According to the Ownership Reform Foundation Act, the municipality may have extended the time limit for operations in the land return procedure by 30 June 2016 at the latest. The decision to compensate for the assets was to be taken by the municipality by 31 December 2016 at the latest. A total of 140500 asset files containing 233400 claims are open in the process of repaying and compensating assets. 99.8% of these claims have now been met, but by the end of 2016, 220 return and compensation files, which contain up to 400 individuals' claims, were not completed. In some counties, all or almost all of the procedures for the return and compensation of property have already been completed. The asset files are closed. Most of the outstanding proceedings have been shut down due to succession or litigation and can be completed after the identification of the heirs or the entry into force of the judgment, but in some cases the current law does not provide a solution to the situation. This means that there will be a legal vacuum. Cases in which the persons entitled could not have carried out the necessary actions within the time limit for reasons beyond their control, i.e. the total number of problematic asset files is around 50. Although there are very few such cases on the scale of the whole ownership and land reform, a proper final solution must also be found to complete them. This bill offers this solution. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U137	The next item is the first reading of the draft law amending the Code of Criminal Procedure and the Act implementing the Code of Criminal Procedure initiated by the Government of the Republic (transposition of the European Investigation Order Directive). Minister of Justice Urmas Reinsalu, please waitress!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U138	Honorable President! A respectable representative of the people! This draft amends the Code of Criminal Procedure in order to implement the European Investigation Order Directive in the Estonian legal order, according to which the submission of legal aid applications between the countries of the European Union, with the exception of Denmark and Ireland, which did not participate in the adoption of this directive, would in future only take place through the submission of a European Investigation Order. This means setting the same time limits and procedural requirements for the transmission of legal aid applications in criminal matters. This is, of course, positive because it ensures good cooperation in criminal proceedings to establish the truth. The aim of the Directive is to allow direct communication between the law enforcement authorities of the Member States in the resolution of requests for mutual assistance. So far, legal aid issues between the countries of the European Union have been resolved in such a way that the role of central authority has been fulfilled by the Ministry of Justice. Now, in the case of Estonia, the prosecutor's office and in other countries, the law enforcement authorities can begin to organise these matters directly. It should also be stressed that most of the procedure requested under the European Investigation Order is carried out in Estonia in accordance with Estonian procedural law and conditions. However, the directive also provides for specific procedural procedures which are mandatory. These are interrogation and transmission of information held by law enforcement authorities and identification of the telephone number or IP address holder. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U139	Any questions for the waitress? No, it's not. Thanks to the waitress! I would like to ask the Chair of the Committee on Legal Affairs for a joint presentation by the Commission. Jaanus Karilaid, please!

ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U148	Today's 19th item is the draft law amending the Nature Conservation Act initiated by the Government of the Republic (Implementing the Nagoya Protocol) 444. Mr. Marko Pomerantsi, Minister for the Environment of the Chair, please waitress for the initiators.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U149	Good morning. [[Laughter. The minister's got the vote off.]] I would like full attention, Mr President-in-Office, to the Chamber. It's not good with the voice, but I'm very good with the bill. This concerns the draft law amending the Nature Conservation Act as regards the implementation of the Nagoya Protocol. They always ask where the mysterious places we ratify the protocols are. In this case, Nagoya, which is located in Japan, between Tokyo and Hiroshima on the Pacific Coast. You have been presented with a draft law amending the Nature Conservation Act, drawn up for inspection under the direction of the Ministry of the Environment and in cooperation with other ministries, which is the basis for implementing Regulation No 511/2014 of the European Union already in force in Estonia on compliance measures for users under the Nagoya Protocol on Access to Genetic Resources and the Fair and Equitable Sharing of Benefits from their Use in the Union. The aim is to ensure that genetic resources and traditional knowledge associated with genetic resources used in the European Union are legally acquired from the country of origin. Simplely, it is almost something like coffee fair trade where coffee comes from, people should also get their share. The amendments concern, in particular, R &amp; D institutions, which must now satisfy themselves and be ready to prove the legitimate acquisition of the genetic material used in their work, i.e. its origin. The Protocol has been created, in particular, for developing countries to protect genetic resources due to the high biodiversity in those countries, in order to prevent potential bio-piracy. Each country has the right to impose a restriction on access to its own genetic resources under the Nagoya Protocol. In order to have the relevant institutional framework in the Estonian state in the form of competent authorities, the Ministry of the Environment, the Ministry of Rural Affairs and the Ministry of Education and Research are designated as national competent authorities, and the obligation to carry out national and administrative supervision is assigned to the Environmental Inspectorate. The European Union Regulation also obliges researchers to submit declarations to competent authorities in their field where they receive funds for research involving the use of genetic resources and associated traditional knowledge and/or at the final stage of the development of a product developed as a result of the use of genetic resources or associated traditional knowledge. These declarations shall be made through the DECLARE database established by the European Commission, which is a user declaration that they exercise due diligence in accordance with the European Union Regulation. The next step is ratification of the Nagoya Protocol. Thank you for your attention.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U150	Thank you, waitress. No questions for you. On behalf of the Commission, I would like to ask Mr Valeri Korb, Member of the Committee on the Environment, for a co-presentation of the Chair.

While 90 monitoring reports were collected before and these data were entered by officials in the environmental register, they are now submitted by the monitoring team itself. A lot of working time is being saved, and we can analyze it nicely on the basis of these monitoring data and draw conclusions about our nature. So good bill. Please support me!

ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U170	Mr. Chairman of the morning session! In the last decades of the Soviet era, there were numerous educational institutions in Estonia that trained middle-level lawyers in particular, who began to act as legal consuls in the institutions, as well as very much in the areas of labour law and labour protection. One of the areas of activity of these people in which they were professional was the question of what right workers have to take leave and how much holiday pay must be. Because the Soviet general legal order had a firm conviction that every person must have the right to paid leave. When Estonia's independence was restored, these people continued their useful activities in all places. This also meant that there were constant problems in the parliamentary assembly, how to set the vacations of members of these assemblies and how to count the fees. I have looked at the materials of the Supreme Council, where this matter was solved relatively simply: a collective holiday was announced. But the situation in the Riigikogu became quite complicated since the VII membership. For many years there was a holiday institution, but the associated financial calculations were quite complex, for example when an extraordinary session fell into summer months. It is also natural from this point of view that the laws on the President of the Republic also included the concept of the President of the Republic's holiday, 35 days a year. As far as I am concerned, no President of the Republic has made himself on vacation for a single day in these years. In February this year, a letter from the Office of the President arrived at the Riigikogu, raising this holiday problem and proposing to consider whether it would not be appropriate to abolish the institution of the President of the Republic's holiday from the law at all and equalize the President with its status as we have in the Riigikogu. We are not on official leave, but this does not mean that we cannot organise one or more sufficiently long rest periods for ourselves a year. On top of this letter, the Committee on Constitutional Affairs ordered an analysis from the Legal and Analytical Department of the Chancellery of the Riigikogu in order to find out what the legal content of this matter is and what the situation is in our neighbouring countries. The Legal and Analytical Department could not establish that there would be an institution of the Head of State's holiday in any country, just as it is currently in Estonian law. Obviously they did not control all the countries either, but at least by name this argument is correct for all our neighbouring countries. It was a surprise to me personally that this analysis quoted a speech that I had given here on the floor in 2001 when the amendment of the laws on the President was discussed. There I said, 16 years ago, that such an institution should not be in law, and I called it a lot of weird. So I am very happy to say, personally, that the talk I spoke about this remote control 16 years ago has matured over 16 years in such a way that the solution to the problem, as I once proposed, is now offered by the people to whom this provision applies. I note that I will probably not be as patient in the coming weeks, months and years as I was 16 years ago because I probably will not have time to wait for any problems until the problem matures in the minds of decision-makers and officials. What else can I say about that? Here's a remarkable thing. In 2006, all laws relating to the President were again dealt with in the Riigikogu, at the head of the law. The draft amendment to this law at that time had been initiated by the three political groups of the coalition at the time. This was the time when Andrus Ansip was the first government to consist of the Reform Party, the Centre Party and the People's Union. The main content of the draft initiated by these three political groups was the adoption of various changes to the benefits granted to the President's spouse. In the course of the proceedings, none of the Commission's rapporteurs or any member of the Riigikogu who was present in the Chamber paid attention to the fact that, among other things, the President's benefits were supplemented by a new paragraph of § 5 of the Act. This was supplemented by paragraph 4 as follows: 'In the event of termination of office, the President shall be entitled to financial compensation for unused leave. " Why would it be necessary? In principle, the presidents could have asked for compensation without it. I think the explanation for this is in Soviet labour law. Soviet labour law provided that if a person does not take his or her leave, either by his or her own will or by reason of any circumstances, there were two options: whether he or she would be given these days of leave later, or whether he or she would receive compensation for unused leave. But Soviet law had set a certain time limit, I suppose it was two years, i.e. it was not possible to wait five years with the use of days without withdrawal, but it had to be taken out sooner. This was also the case with cash benefits. What can I conclude from this? I couldn't control everything from the archives, but it seems that President Lennart Meril never thought that he could take compensation. This law was adopted in February 2006. And 2006 was the year when President Rüütel's mandate ended in autumn. I suppose President Rüütel received compensation for his five years of unused leave, and I think the next President Toomas Hendrik Ilves also received compensation at the end of his two term of office. How big were they? The amount of the last compensation that President Ilves now received when President Kaljulaid came to office was communicated to us. It was about EUR 31000. So in earlier times, as the President's salary was constantly rising, as members of the Riigikogu depended on the average income in Estonia, the compensations for the past five years were probably somewhat lower. I suppose that's the case, but since I haven't personally investigated the archives, it's kind of hard to say. A number of phases were being discussed in the Committee on Constitutional Affairs. First, there was the phase of the problem. The Commission then quite unanimously considered that a draft had to be initiated. The draft is minimalist and takes everything from the two laws on presidents that it says about leave. All decisions were taken quite unanimously in the Commission, i.e. no one called for a vote on any decision. It is usually the custom of the Riigikogu to call such a decision consensual. I'm not sure this term is the most accurate and the best, but there were no problems. So the Commission's proposal to you is to finish the first reading of the draft and set the deadline for amendments on the 25th day of May. Thank you for your attention.

The guardians of the bill did not agree that it would take us back somewhere, but felt that it would strengthen our position. What else can I tell you here? The basic overview of the principle of uniformity was given by Jüri Adams, who explained that the concept of uniformity means that everyone who participates in the elections has the weight of one vote equal to that of the others. He was accepted by Arto Aas. In principle, that concludes the draft debate more or less. Kalle Laane felt that, just in case, you should definitely ask the Chancellor of Justice for an opinion. ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U195	Thanks to the waitress! No questions for you. On behalf of the Estonian Free Party Group, Jüri Adams, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U196	Mr President of the sitting! Ladies and gentlemen of the House! The view of the Free Party Group is that, if any changes are made in the field of electronic voting, only in order to extend this possibility, also in time. We certainly do not support the idea of narrowing down the conditions currently in force. In this context, the Free Party Group proposes to reject this draft at first reading. Now I would like to spend a few more minutes of your morning's expensive time on one of the problems relating to this bill, which we must all be aware of. This is about the fact that our constitution says that elections in Estonia must be uniform. In Estonia, there have been very many statements of opinion in the last few months that we use voting on paper on election day, i.e. on the ballot paper, but at the same time electronic voting is also possible. It should be pointed out that such a situation is contrary to our constitution. How can it be said that these two very different methods of voting are uniform with each other? For me, the most embarrassing thing is that none of our professors of political or administrative law have spoken or explained to the people what the problem is. Similarly, people in the electoral service of the Chancellery of the Riigikogu have had their mouths full of water. Rather, I have the impression that, for certain historical reasons, these people have probably never heard a professor at any school talk about where the word 'one' comes from and what it means. My explanation is that the political lyrics came up with the corresponding word in French and originally in French. In French it is in the form of égal, which means equal, and for the first time it was probably introduced in the first constitution of the French Republic, which was adopted in 1791. I didn't check it out, maybe it was already inside the Constitution of the French kingdom, but I still think it was first in the Constitution of 1791. The constitutions of all European countries, including those of Estonia, have been written in imitation of and to a large extent repeating the basic standards laid down in this French constitution of 1791. These basic standards for elections in the French Republic were such that elections must be general in French on the universe, which means that all people must be able to choose. Secondly, they must be secret, or secret, which means that it must be possible to give their voices in such a way that outsiders do not see what the content of that voice is. Third, they have to be an égal. Now the question is, what does that mean? This égal has a very clear meaning in its time, namely that one person's voice must have the exact same value as another person's. We will take other languages that we are more familiar with in Estonia. For example, the German current constitution says that elections must be gleich, which is in German 'equal'. The Finnish Constitution says that everyone must have a yhtäläinen eunioikeus. The current Russian constitution clearly states that everyone must have a ravnoje izberatelnoje pravo. More time, please.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U197	Please, three more minutes!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U198	I don't know who introduced the word 'one-type' in Estonian in Estonia. Maybe it wasn't a happy choice, but we can't change that in retrospect right now. Our main problem is that before the Russian Revolution in 1917 all elections in Estonia were such that they did not meet this criterion of uniformity. In the cities, the vicious elections were on the basis of wealth, and we had very non-same elections, i.e. each farmer had one vote, but the servants and the nomads received one vote at ten. Since 1917 there has been no experience in Estonia that we have had elections on which we can say that they are not uniform. And in Estonia, both theorists, lawyers and electoral practitioners have forgotten that this word has had historical meaning, that it is a term, and that it cannot be interpreted as 'one-size-fits-all' in Estonian. Each country has a number of different technical means of elections, even if some of them are needed for the various elections for people with physical disabilities, and there are also technically different options. The fact that there are only two electoral methods is quite unusual, as there are mostly more than two electoral methods in the country. If we have one vote in each person's choice, it will undoubtedly comply with this principle of uniformity of the French Constitution. If, for example, the choice of the German Bundestag has two votes, then it corresponds in the same way because it is equally uniform. I'd like to invite you, esteemed ambassadors. We have so many people confused with the meaning of this concept of uniformity. If you meet a person who doesn't understand the quite simple meaning of this special term, please help him! Let us work together to make this clear, and let us not confuse our citizens more than they already are! Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U289	Honourable Riigikogu! Good Chairman of the Riigikogu! Before you at first reading is the draft decision of the Riigikogu 451 'Use of the Defence Forces in the fulfilment of the international obligations of the Estonian State in the training programme for supporting the defence capabilities of Iraq led by the North Atlantic Treaty Organisation'. According to this draft, pursuant to § 128 (1) of the Constitution of the Republic of Estonia and § 34 (1) of the National Defence Act, the Riigikogu decides, first, to use up to five active troops of the Defence Forces from the entry into force of this Decision until 31 December 2017 in the training programme to support the reconstruction of the defence capabilities of Iraq led by the North Atlantic Treaty Organisation. The second point is that the ceiling of the Defence Forces personnel set out in paragraph 1 of this Decision may be increased during the rotation period to 10 active troops within 30 days of the arrival in Iraq of the active troops taking part in the rotation. It is a draft that requires two readings and gives the relevant mandate. Now, from the background of this draft decision. In 2017, NATO launched a training programme to support the reconstruction of Iraqi defence capabilities, which in English sounds like Defence Capacity Building...
ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U290	Excuse me, Minister. [[The manager's ringing the bell.]]
ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U291	I'm sorry to interrupt your conversation, but let's make this decision. Thank you very much, Chairman. DCB is the abbreviation we'll use later. It provides precisely for small training teams to be deployed to Iraq for a short period of up to two months, consisting of five to ten active troops. The training programme is a NATO-led activity aimed at the alliance against threats from the south. Estonia's participation in the programme is in line with our security and foreign policy interests. Indeed, as you well know, we welcome NATO units in the Baltic States and Poland here. A major exercise is currently taking place in the Spring Storm, where NATO units are also participating. And in order to demonstrate NATO's unity, and also to ensure real unity, Estonia contributes to all its missions to ensure security elsewhere, along with its allies. Then the allies will do the same here on our border and on NATO's eastern border. Within this DCB, Estonia, in cooperation with other NATO countries, is preparing to contribute in two areas: military medicine and the fight against improvised explosive devices. In these two areas, the Iraqi security forces have the most critical capacity gaps, for which Iraq has formally requested the support of NATO countries. We therefore need to deal with these problems in cooperation with our allies, including in one of their main focal points in Iraq. Now Estonia's contribution. I'm asking you for a mandate to send up to five active troops on a mission. Our real plan is to send in one person, but if it is necessary, the mandate until the end of the year would be to increase this real contribution. There is no need to explain to you, good Riigikogu, the importance of participating in these missions. It is necessary to ensure our own security. I want to thank all our active troops, the mission soldiers who have been on missions over the years and who are there now. They serve the interests of our own country. It's gonna cost the country too. Our calculation is as follows: The costs of participating in the operation in 2017 are calculated at EUR 43000 and are covered by the resources provided by the Riigikogu in the State Budget Act to the Ministry of Defence, i.e. the same 2%, part of which is intended to cover these costs in order to send our active troops to the mission. ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U293	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, As the Minister has already said, the draft submitted by the Government of the Republic envisages the participation of up to five Estonian active troops in a training programme to support the reconstruction of the defence capabilities of Iraq led by NATO, called Defence Capacity Building. This is a non-combat programme aimed at NATO from the south, particularly against potential threats from the Middle East. The training programme shall provide for the deployment of small training teams with rotation periods of up to two months to Iraq. They contribute to the training of local military personnel, as the Minister has already mentioned, in the fight against military medicine and improvised explosive devices. Indeed, in these two areas there is the most critical capacity gap in the Iraqi security forces, which the Iraqi Government has formally requested NATO assistance to address. The new programme, launched in NATO at the meeting on 8 May, was presented to the members of the Commission by representatives of the Ministry of Defence's administration, including the Deputy Chancellor of Justice and Administration in the capacity of the Deputy Chancellor of Defence Policy Erki Kodar. The programme envisages sending small units to Iraq for a short period of time. This is a mission on the same legal basis as the international military operation Inherent Resolve, which was launched last year and led by the US, in which Estonia is already training Iraqi military personnel. The difference is that the framework for the new operation is different. As this program is still under development, the mission would initially be attended by a military officer, an explosive expert. The full-scale training programme will start in September of this year, according to current plans. In order to show solidarity with NATO's new and necessary undertaking, Estonia aims to join it as soon as possible. Thus, on the basis of this draft, Estonia fulfils its responsibilities arising from NATO membership. Procedural decisions. At the meeting of 8 May this year, the National Defence Committee decided by consensus to propose to the Board of the Riigikogu that the draft under discussion be included on the agenda for the first reading of the meeting of the Riigikogu on 17 May and to propose that the first reading of the draft be closed. The rapporteur for the Commission was appointed here. At this meeting, the National Defence Committee also decided by consensus to propose to the Chairman of the Riigikogu that the deadline for submitting amendments be set. 5.15 p.m. on May 31st. Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U295	The next item is the first reading of draft 403 of the Riigikogu's decision to propose to the Government of the Republic to suspend Estonia's participation in the refugee redistribution plan. The initiating waitress is Jaak Madison. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U296	Honourable Chairman of the Riigikogu! Ladies and gentlemen, Good guests on the balcony! It is true that on 20 March the s. a the Conservative People's Party has submitted a draft proposal to the Government of the Republic to suspend Estonia's participation in the plan for the redistribution of refugees. Specifically, the purpose of this draft. To begin with, Estonia will participate in the redistribution plan agreed on 22 September 2015 at the Justice and Home Affairs Council of the European Union. In doing so, the Republic of Estonia undertook to relocate and settle 553 people from Greece, Italy and Turkey. This decision had been given a mandate at the meeting of the European Union Affairs Commission on 10 September 2015, according to which Estonia prefers mutual assistance on a voluntary basis. The purpose of this draft can be commented on in such a way that, as Estonia has been one of the most advanced countries in fulfilling its voluntary obligation to relocate and settle refugees, 140 persons have arrived in Estonia. As the Government of the Republic of Estonia has emphasised the voluntary participation in this mechanism, Estonia can also voluntarily suspend its implementation. One reason undoubtedly is the incident that took place six months ago, where in a refugee family that arrived in Estonia, the man tied his husband up and threatened to set his wife on fire. He's currently under trial for attempted murder and rape. In the second case, the husband set fire to a husband who, to this day, lies in intensive care with unconsciousness and severe burns. This man has been detained and is awaiting trial. In order to ensure the security of Estonian people and to avoid cultural contradictions, it is reasonable to suspend the reception of refugees for an indefinite period of time. Poland does not participate in the mechanism for the redistribution of refugees in the European Union, which has challenged the relevant decision before the European Court of Justice on the ground that it is a compulsory quota system. If it were to assist each other on a voluntary basis, Poland's decision would not have been justified. According to current information, the court settlement should come by the end of this year. It should also be noted that, in recent days, pressure has been increased by the European Commissioner for Migration, Dimitris Avramopoulos, who is threatening Poland and Hungary with sanctions if these countries voluntarily refuse to assist Greece, Italy and Turkey and accept migrants within the quota allocated to them. Nor is social impact of little importance. According to studies carried out by Kantar Emor, Turku Research AS and the Institute of Social Studies, the Estonian public does not support immigration in the way it has been supported in Western Europe and the Nordic countries. The Riigikogu received two petitions from citizens between 2015 and 2016, the first with 19000 signatures and the second with 40,000 signatures. The suspension of the reception of refugees would therefore significantly alleviate societal tensions. It should be noted that the draft was also on the table in the Committee on European Union Affairs for an opinion. There it was discussed for quite a while and a decision was also made which needed to be voted on in order to give guidance to the Committee on Constitutional Affairs. According to the vote at the meeting of the Committee on European Union Affairs, only one Member of the Commission, the speaker here, supported the approval of the draft, all others, including the Group of the Isamaa and Res Publica Union. It should certainly be mentioned that the introduction of the Conservative People's Party Group's draft gave an article of opinion by the honourable colleague Kalle Muuli a few days before the submission of this draft in mid-March. According to him, Estonia should suspend its participation in the current quota system. Since we saw that our ideas were also to be welcomed by the coalition ambassadors, we considered it necessary to come halfway and submit a draft and give other members of the Fatherland and Res Publica Association the opportunity to support the draft. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U297	Questions. There are no questions for the originator. Thank you. The rapporteur for the Constitutional Commission is Mart Nutt. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U298	Mr. Chairman! Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! This draft was discussed by the Committee on Constitutional Affairs at its regular meeting on 9 May. In addition to the members of the Constitutional Committee of the Riigikogu and Henn Põlluaasa, the advisers of the committee and the press advisor of the Public Relations Department of the Chancellery of the Riigikogu Eva Vahur were present at the meeting of the committee. To begin with, the draft was presented by Henn Põlluaas, on behalf of the EKRE Group, as I said, who presented basically the same arguments as the previous speaker. That is why I am not going to repeat them, but I am going to turn to the reasoning. The first question was asked by Riigikogu member Peeter Ernits of the Centre Party, who noted that on 22 September 2017 two years have passed since the approval of the redistribution mechanism, and in fact only a quarter of the refugees who are scheduled to receive have actually arrived in Estonia during this period. Do the drafters have any information on whether there will be many more refugees coming to Estonia in the near future? Henn Põllaas replied that refugees were definitely coming, and officials from the Police and Border Guard Board were in the process of selecting them. There was no mention of any number and more secure process, but he mentioned that, despite the choice to be made, Estonia is unable to exercise substantive control over these people. They are not subject to any background data, nor are there any databases to check them. Mart Nutt said that Mr Põljaas has highlighted very important problems, but they largely go beyond the framework of this draft. If you look at the number of people affected by this proposal, it is relatively small, especially when compared to how many immigrants come to Estonia every year. That's what Mr. Pier said. Chairman of the committee Ken-Marti Vaher pointed out that in 2016 the Riigikogu adopted the law amending the law on granting international protection to foreigners and related amendments to other laws. This law introduced changes concerning the resettlement of refugees from outside the European Union and the relocation of refugees from other Member States of the European Union. The faction of the Estonian Conservative People's Party was also in favour of these changes, i.e. it voted in favour of them. I also note Mr Adams' statement. He pointed out that the European Union's open door policy was, in principle, retreating and that it was positive. At the same time, the European Union has failed to do anything, to shape any policy that would reduce the risks of possible next mass migration. Following this debate, the Committee on Constitutional Affairs took procedural decisions. First, to put a draft of 403 on the agenda of the plenary assembly today, 17 May (the decision was taken by consensus); second, to propose a final vote on the draft (also by consensus) and to appoint Mart Nutt, member of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs (exactly by consensus). I will also read out who was part of the consensus: these were members of the Riigikogu Ken-Marti Vaher. Mart Nutt, Anneli Ott, Ivari Padar, Peeter Ernits, Henn Põlluaas, Helmut Hallemaa, Jüri Adams, Arto Aas, Lauri Luik and Kalle Laanet. I would like to remind you that the draft requires a majority of its component members for adoption. Thank you.

This would be primarily an amendment to the two paragraphs of the Law, which are § 49 and § 50. One relates to the revocation of refugee status or refugee status and the other to the expulsion of an alien under certain conditions. The substance of the matter is that these amendments are also driven by these same cases. If this is an extremely lawless person who has been granted refugee status in Estonia, then there should be an opportunity to withdraw this status in a much simpler way than has been the case so far. Indeed, the current regime provides that a refugee must have committed a first-degree crime, so it is possible to withdraw refugee status from him. Through the Ministry of Justice, it was proposed that refugee status can also be lifted for other serious crimes, such as crimes against individuals, drug crimes, crimes against children and other misconducts, which pose a serious threat to public order. However, this must also be accompanied by a state assessment that this activity really poses a threat to the public policy of the State. In the event of a security threat, it is still possible to withdraw this status. So yesterday, in the Committee on Legal Affairs, this issue was discussed again. Yesterday, the Committee on Legal Affairs decided that, on 29 May, after we return from a week without sitting, we will re-table this issue. Can I have a few more minutes, please?

ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U335	The next item is the second reading of the draft law amending the Law on excise duties on alcohol, tobacco, fuel and electricity and tobacco law initiated by the Government of the Republic. The Commission's waitress is Priit Sibul. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U336	Honourable Chairman of the Riigikogu! Ladies and gentlemen, I would remind you that the government initiated this draft on 6 February and the main changes by 2018 are the taxation of alternative tobacco products by excise duty, the increase in excise duty rates for cigars and cigarillos and the conversion of their excise duty rate into two components, which depends on volume and price, as is currently the case for cigarettes, the granting of excise duty to the consignee of the goods at a distance, and the reduction of the administrative burden on economic operators on their accounts. An alternative tobacco product is, for excise purposes, different from a classic tobacco product with a nicotine content or no nicotine content, which is used in the same way as a tobacco product or for the same purpose. Alternative products are e-cigarette filling fluid, steam stones replacing water pipe tobacco and the liquid used to season them, as well as steam stones themselves and other alternative tobacco products that are not solid or liquid but, for example, gel. Alternative tobacco products are subject to excise duty in order to eliminate unequal competition between alternative and classic tobacco products in order to prevent the loss of excise revenue as a result of the replacement of the consumption of conventional tobacco products by alternative products and in order to protect public health. The bill was in this Chamber at first reading on 22 February, and that is why I recall what we discussed quite a while ago. By the deadline for the tabling of amendments, 9 March, we received nine amendments, two of which were withdrawn by the authors, because they concerned an explanatory memorandum, not a draft, and we do not normally change the explanatory memorandum. Some of the amendments made by the same author were essentially related and such amendments were included by the Commission in the list as the basis points for the amendment, with the agreement of the latter. After the first reading, we have discussed this draft at five meetings: 21 March, 4 April, 19, 15 and 16 May. On 21 March, we had a meeting with stakeholders, where the Estonian Association of Tobacco Manufacturers, Philip Morris Eesti OÜ, AS Rasa Trading Company, OÜ Sigari Maja and Estonian Chamber of Commerce and Industry were represented. Representatives of the Ministry of Social Affairs and the Ministry of Finance were also present at the meeting. As an expert, Dr. Ülle Ani, medical lecturer of the lung clinic at the University of Tartu Hospital, was involved. During this debate, we discussed assumptions and impact analyses concerning the taxation of e-cigarettes, the definitions of alternative tobacco products, the principles of excise duty on alternative tobacco products and excise duties, as well as changes in excise duties on cigars and cigarillos. The amendments received to the Riigikogu from the address of the interest groups were not supported by the commission, sticking to the draft and the views expressed in the explanatory memorandum of the initiator. By way of derogation, the Commission considered it necessary to clarify the issue of heated tobacco products. The Committee on Finance itself introduced two amendments, one of which improves legal clarity in the provisions on heated tobacco products, as I said, and the other simplifies the rules on excise warehouses in very small packages for the production of food supplements. Explanations of amendments are included in the list of amendments. The lead committee proposed to conclude the second reading by consensus and, if the second reading is completed, the final debate on the draft could take place on 31 May. Perhaps in a few words that the main changes that we discussed in addition to the products to be heated were proposals to reduce the excise duty rate on e-cigarettes. The government proposes to set a rate of EUR 0,2 per ml. We had a debate on whether to tax only nicotine fluids or all liquids. We have come to the understanding that we are sticking to the taxation of all liquids. The second issue was that perhaps we are setting too high a rate of excise duty if we opt for the EUR 0.2. There are proposals to initially tax it at a zero rate or at a rate of EUR 0.11 per ml. As I said, the Commission maintained that the proposed rate in the draft from the Government is appropriate. We discussed at length, in three or more sessions, these heated tobacco products. Indeed, they are different and different ministries had a different understanding of whether they fit into the excise law at the moment. I think that we finally achieved a consensual understanding with both the officials and the members of the Commission and supplemented the definition of cigarettes and smoking tobacco in such a way that it has now been extended to heated products. It is now unambiguous to all parties and regulated by law. These were the main points of discussion. Finally, we decided that the second reading could be concluded today and on 31 May, on Wednesday of our next working week, the draft could be put to the final vote. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U379	Fifth item: Second reading of Bill 374 of the Customs Act initiated by the Government of the Republic. Mr. Erki Savisaare, member of the Economic Commission for the Chair, would you like to speak?
ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U380	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, The Committee on Economic Affairs discussed this draft on 11 May and, first of all, let me remind you a little bit about what this is about. The Government of the Republic initiated the draft Customs Act on 23 January of this year. The purpose of the draft is to bring customs legislation into line with the new customs legislation of the European Union. The provisions repeating European Union legislation are deleted from the law and the terminology is amended. From 1 May 2016, the new Customs Code will be in force in the European Union, harmonising customs rules within the Union. For the trader, customs procedures will be easier and paperless. The main objective of the new Code is to establish uniform requirements and to bring customs clearance into a new, uniform electronic environment in all Member States of the European Union. The electronic environment will be set up gradually by 2020, with transitional provisions being implemented in 2016 to 202020. As the volume of the amendments is large, a new complete text of the Customs Act was drawn up for the sake of the readability of the law. The draft does not change the duties of the Tax and Customs Board in the implementation of customs legislation. The first reading of the bill took place on 22 March, the deadline for submitting amendments, by 5 April at 5 p.m., the members of the Riigikogu, the political groups and other committees did not submit proposals. The Committee on Economic Affairs discussed the preparation for the second reading at its meeting on 11 May, which was attended by a representative of the Ministry of Finance. The Commission decided to propose 11 amendments to the draft, nine of which were submitted to the Committee on Economic Affairs by the Ministry of Finance. All the proposals were supported by consensus within the Commission on the basis of the reasons set out in the list of amendments. Most of the changes were of a technical nature. The Committee on Economic Affairs decided to submit the draft for second reading on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu by 17 May and to propose that the second reading be terminated. It was also decided that the final vote on the draft should the second reading be closed on 31 May. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U381	There's no questioners on the board, but I'm still gonna ask the waitress if there are any questions? No, it's not. Thank you, dear waitress. I'm opening negotiations. The remaining amendments have also been tabled by the Committee on Economic Affairs: 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10 and 11. We have examined the amendments together. The proposal of the lead committee is to conclude the second reading of draft 374. Second reading is over.
ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U382	The sixth item on the agenda is the draft law 188 on the amendment of the Public Transport Act, Traffic Act and State Duty Act initiated by the members of the Riigikogu Einar Vallbaumi, Ivari Padari, Vilja Toomasti, Andre Sepa, Kalle Palling, Johannes Kerdi, Remo Holsmer, Jüri Jaanson, Ken-Marti Vaheri, Meelis Mälberg, Laine Randjärve, Anne Sulling, Marko Mihkelson, Urve Tiidus, Toomas Kivimägi, Jaanus Marrandi, Deniss Borodichi, Heidy Purga, Keit Pentus-Rosimand, Tanel Winter, Eerik-Niles Krossi, Kalle Laaneti, Igor Gräzini, Juhan Partsi, Kristjan Kõlljala and Martin Kuke. Today's the second reading. I would like to present the chair of the Vice-Chairman of the Economic Commission, Toomas Kivimägi!
ParlaMint-EE_2017-05-17_U383	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! Indeed, this is a bill initiated by 26 members of the Riigikogu already on 25 February 2016. So it's been over a year since we've reached second reading. But from the very beginning, I dare say that this has come to the fore: good news is coming. By the deadline for submitting amendments, 10 May 2016, a total of 21 proposals were received from members and political groups of the Riigikogu. Over the course of more than a year since the first reading, the Commission has been approached and proposed by various organisations. We have, if you count, 25 letters of these opinions. The number of these organisations is somewhat less than 25, as some organisations have expressed their views several times. For the sake of saving your time, I'm not going to read that long list. I do consider it very important to emphasise the cooperation between the first and second readings of the Economic Commission with representatives of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications, as well as very good cooperation with the Counsellor of the Ministry of Justice, Tax and Customs Board, specialists of the Tallinn Transport Board, as well as the Office of the Chancellor of Justice. During the discussion on the preparation of amendments in the lead committee, a total of six working group meetings took place and there were nine meetings of the Economic Commission, where the subject was discussed. As a result, an amendment amending the wording of the entire draft was formulated by the Commission. The new wording came about in such a way that the Committee on Economic Affairs developed substantive principles and guidelines and subsequently replaced the old text with a new one. I once again congratulate the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications, especially I would like to thank Hindrek Allveed and Madis Ehastu, as well as the secretariat of the Committee on Economic Affairs of Piia Schults, who did a very, very good job indeed. As I said, a total of 21 amendments were received after the first reading, plus one proposed by the Committee on Economic Affairs itself. At the meeting of the Commission on 11 May 2017, representatives of the Centre Party and the Fatherland and Res Publica Union withdrew their amendments, not because they were neither substantive nor constructive, but because most of the content of these amendments was reflected in the Commission's amendment. There were five amendments on the table: one was tabled by the Committee on Economic Affairs and four by Mr Ammas. Since the amendments tabled by Mr Ammas did not have the authority to withdraw from the committee, and perhaps Mr Andres would not have wanted them, the Commission discussed them and voted on them. But let's get to the point. Amendment 1 was tabled by the Committee on Economic Affairs and its content is indeed to replace the whole text of the draft with a new one. I would like to say what is usually said at the end of the report as a matter of urgency: that the Commission decided to propose that the second reading be interrupted and that everyone should be given the opportunity to table amendments. Therefore, everyone in this Chamber has the opportunity to get a proper look at this draft and to table amendments again. I think this is well-founded, because indeed, the text of the draft law, as well as the principle approach, has changed very significantly. The most important, fundamental change in the starting point is that, while the original draft was intended to regulate the carriage of goods by means of an agreement with a separate regulation, the principle underlying the amendment proposed by the Committee on Economic Affairs is that the carriage of goods by road is also the carriage of goods by road. You could say it's one of the subspecies of taxi transport. Figuratively speaking, as of tomorrow, there would be no reason to talk about two different things - taxi and freight. According to the new draft, in both cases it is a taxi service and it makes sense therefore that regulation is largely the same. Just one example: the authorisation requirements are essentially completely identical. Currently, taxi transport is subject to a taxi driving licence, a passenger card and a driver's card, and all three of them are also required for conventional services. There are no differences in content. However, the differences are in the form, since the transport licence, the passenger card and the vehicle card are electronic in the case of conventional services, and the passenger card is tangible in the case of taxi transport. When you drive in the cab, I'm sure you've noticed it, it's got a picture of the driver, and it's got some information on it. Contract services do not require such a paper-based service provider to hold such a card. Another important change and a fundamental change in position is that the requirements for taxi transport are significantly relaxed. This is due to three factors. The first reason why the requirements for the carriage of taxis had to be relaxed is that the two initially different modes of transport, the conventional transport and the taxi transport would fit into a single box. Secondly, taxi drivers have long said that a number of requirements are rudiments and basically do not guarantee anything. Thirdly, the best guarantee of quality is fierce competition and, if this bill is approved, close competition is ensured. This is much better in terms of quality assurance than the limits and requirements laid down by the legislator in the law. What are the most important reliefs for taxi transport? The first very fundamental, one might say, even a revolutionary change is that taxiing is no longer restricted to a service area. It might be a little incomprehensible. In fact, taxi transport is generally limited to the administrative territory of the municipality which has issued it a transfer licence, a service card and a vehicle card. The law, however, made it possible to allow the provision of services in the territory of another municipality if the local governments cooperated and agreed to do so. I assume that Tallinn has such agreements with neighbouring municipalities. But it is actually incomprehensible that if you take it very strictly, for example, if you want to drive across the border from Pärnu to Sauga, it is as if you should not have such a taxi service. Now this restriction has disappeared, which means that current taxi drivers can also provide taxi services throughout Estonia, in any city or municipality. It is important that they comply with the requirements for taxi transport in the local government. This has probably not been done on a very large scale, but Tallinn, for example, has introduced quality requirements for taxi transport in the so-called euro class and some other requirements. Compliance with them is also mandatory if taxi transport is carried out in the administrative territory of another municipality. Another fundamental change is the abolition of the requirement for professional training for taxi drivers. This, too, seems to be a rudiment. I think the carriers themselves are interested in carrying out initial training. At the same time, it has been some expense, perhaps just over 100 euros, which taxi drivers have had to bear, and it also takes 30 hours. We believe that this has been unjustified regulation by the legislator. Thus, the requirement for professional training for taxi drivers disappears. Thirdly, the provision of taxi services is made flexible. It's also very revolutionary. I hesitated about this at the first moment, but this bill also grants the right to transport to current taxis on the basis of the information society platform. Taxi can pick up a person who pays by taximeter, but the same taxi can also provide a service on the basis of the information society platform, in which case the calculation is based not on a taximeter, but on the maximum price fixed and accepted on the basis of the information society service platform. This is also a fundamental change. I think that the rudiment is also that the deadline for issuing a taxi licence is 30 days and the deadline for issuing a passenger card is 15 days. The draft proposal is that both should be issued within seven days. In fact, thanks to the development of registers, it has become a very formal procedure. Rather, the entries in the register should be checked and the requirements for issuing one or the other card set out in the Public Transport Act should be checked. If the requirements are met, the cards may be issued. There is no reason to allow the carrier to wait 30 or 15 days. As far as the vehicle card is concerned, it is already subject to a seven-day period of issue. Now some more emphasis on the new text. First of all, the title of the bill has also changed. The amendment of the Traffic Act and the State Fees Act has been added so that the new title is the "Law amending the Public Transport Act, the Traffic Act and the State Fees Act'. It is specified what is not taxi transport. This is Section 3 (2) of the Public Transport Act: the carriage of passengers by passenger cars carried out by the transport operator on a planned route for private purposes, the purpose of which is not to generate revenue, is not considered to be a taxi transport. An example is the case where students fit in with the team to travel from Pärnu to Tallinn. One has a car, and if he wants to share his driving costs in any way, my boyfriend has often used it, then he has a perfectly legitimate right to call his friends and acquaintances who may be interested. It is not considered to be a taxi transport, and it is not covered by the Public Transport Act, and I do not think the tax administration is interested. I have already mentioned that § 57 (1) (2) of the Public Transport Act will be abolished and the requirement for the professional training of taxi drivers will also disappear. The next amendment is that taxi services may also be provided by taxis with no meter. There is no need to have a taximeter if a taxi is ordered and the price is calculated through an information society service. So whoever doesn't want to drive a cab doesn't have to get a meter. Anyone who wants to pick up people at a taxi stop or get customers through a subscription centre, it has to get a meter. As far as the price of driving is concerned, it is known that in some municipalities maximum prices are set for taxis. Where the ordering and pricing of a taxi is carried out through the information society service, the carrier must inform the driver of the driver's first and last name, the number of the passenger card and the maximum cost of the journey, including taxes, before the start of the journey. The photograph must also be transmitted. If the destination of the journey is not fixed in advance, the passenger must be informed of the price components, the tariffs on which the price is calculated or the other basis for calculating the price in such a way as to enable the passenger to calculate the final price of the journey with sufficient accuracy. As a general rule, where a destination is known, the maximum cost of the journey must be known for the journey ordered through the information society service. If no one knows the destination, then indeed no one can calculate the price of the journey, then the basis for calculating the charge must be known. There is another important change that concerns classic taxi transport. Currently, only two tariffs are available for taxi transport: daily tariff and night rate. Taxi drivers have repeatedly expressed the view that there should be greater flexibility in this respect. According to the draft amendment, it is possible to set tariffs on the basis of some other time criteria, in addition to the daily and night rates. However, this can be done by the local government. For example, local authorities have the right to set a weekend tariff or some other tariff, even on New Year's Eve and 1 January. This competence is, of course, the local government. But this flexibility is provided for. All that I have been talking about so far was Amendment 1. Now, however, the following amendments, which remained on the table: Amendment No 2 was tabled by Mr Ammas and its content was that the convention operator should be subject to the same requirements as the taxi operator. Today, I can confirm that these requirements are the same, but somehow the Commission decided not to take this proposal into account. However, I dare say that Mr Ammas' proposal has been taken into account in substance. Amendment 3 was of a technical nature. This, too, was decided not to be taken into account by the commission. Amendment 4 concerns requirements for vehicles. These requirements are also equivalent, even in the case of roadworthiness tests. Even now, cars that carry taxis have to be screened every year. This requirement can also apply to people who carry out taxi transport on the basis of their own private car or on the basis of an information platform in the form of a service. Amendment 5 is also on equivalent requirements. I dare say that all the proposals made by Mr Ammas have been taken into account in substance in Commission Amendment 1, which deals with the text as a whole. Finally, procedural decisions. At the meeting of the Economic Commission on 11 May 2017, it was proposed that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda on 17 May 2017, the decision being consensual. Secondly, it was decided to propose the suspension of the second reading, which was also consensual. Thirdly, it was decided to propose that the deadline for amendments be set at 17 p.m. on 31 May 2017. This decision was also consensual. Thank you!
